    "newString": "\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$ (seed~42), ISR causes PR collapse to 18\\% with 78\\% H1 embedding (vs.\\ 48\\% PR for random), completing the Goodhart trajectory: low-dose gaming ($\\beta{=}0.1$) gives way to high-dose collapse~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) across seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\\citep{folio} subset held constant across conditions. %% data: C001\nEvaluation uses 100 held-out examples with greedy decoding; cross-run ISR values are computed uniformly by \\texttt{eval\\_sta.py}. %% C004\nWe report PR, proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$), hacking rates (H7, H1\\footnote{H1/H7 numbering follows our taxonomy (Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}).}), and non-hacking rate.\\footnote{Non-hacking: not matching any of seven identified patterns (Table~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); does not imply semantic correctness.}\nFull hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:setup}.\n\n\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\n\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\\%$\\to$96\\% while proved-only ISR falls 0.642$\\to$0.003 over 1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\n\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces exec-only GRPO (seed~42): PR rises 32\\%$\\to$93\\%$\\to$96\\% while ISR$^*$ falls $0.642{\\to}0.132{\\to}0.003$ over 1000 steps. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42\nBy step 500, 87.1\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion (H1); by 1000, H1${=}100$\\% with mean 18.55 components.\nThe monotonic ISR$^*$ decline shows extended training worsens structural degeneration invisible to PR.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\\%, H1${=}87.1$\\%); s43 collapses to PR${=}13$\\% via repetition (87\\% hit max length); s44 reaches PR${=}98$\\% via tautological collapse (H7${=}100$\\%). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44\nDespite PR$\\in[13,98]$\\%, all three exhibit structural degeneration detectable by ISR; the $\\sigma{=}\\pm47$\\,pp cross-seed variance shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes (per-seed: Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\n\n\\textbf{SFT baseline.}\nSFT achieves PR${=}14$\\% with ISR$^*{=}0.571$: 92.9\\% non-hacking, only 1/14 exhibits H1, zero H7. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\nThe near-absence of hacking is consistent with hacking being RL-specific; a 100-step RL checkpoint analysis controlling for the PR-level confound is in Appendix~\\ref{app:100step}.\n\n\\subsection{Meta-Level Goodhart's Law}\n\\label{sec:goodhart}\n\nISR-as-reward provides the first empirical demonstration of Goodhart's Law at the meta level~\\citep{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}: the diagnostic itself becomes the target of gaming when optimized.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ & \\textbf{PR\\%}$\\uparrow$ & \\textbf{ISR}$^*$ & \\textbf{H7\\%} & \\textbf{H1\\%} & \\textbf{N-H\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase (0-shot) & --- & 12 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: sta-phase0-baseline\nSFT & --- & 14 & 0.571 & 0 & 7 & \\textbf{93} \\\\ %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\n\\midrule\nExec-only$^\\dagger$ & --- & 93 & 0.132 & 0 & 87 & 13 \\\\ %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2\nISR & 0.1 & 77{\\scriptsize$\\pm$14} & $\\ddagger$ & \\textbf{65} & 17 & ${\\sim}$16 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44\nRandom & 0.1 & \\textbf{85}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$6} & 0.086{\\scriptsize$\\pm$.04} & 0 & 81 & 19 \\\\ %% registry: plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\n\\midrule\nISR$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 18 & 0.143 & 0 & 78 & 22 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42 (single-seed)\nRandom$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 48 & 0.094 & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: plan017-additive-random-s42 (single-seed)\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Goodhart landscape across reward conditions. 3-seed rows show mean$\\pm$std. $^\\dagger$Seed~42; see \\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). $^\\S$$\\beta{=}0.3$: single-seed observation (seed~42). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across available seeds. Best PR and N-H\\% bolded per group.}\n\\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.\nAt $\\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds are dominated by H7 (64.5\\% of proved samples, 149/231) vs.\\ zero H7 under random controls (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively ISR-induced. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\nConclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\\%: random produces only H1, while ISR converts H1$\\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\nRandom also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\\pm$6\\% vs 77$\\pm$14\\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means\n\n\\textbf{Premise-count confound.}\nThe only ISR seed not dominated by H7 (s42) shows higher raw ISR$^*$ (0.415 vs 0.131), but this is entirely a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (5.58 vs 8.46 components), biasing LOO upward. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42, plan018-additive-random-b01-s42\nRestricting to non-hacking samples with $\\geq$3 premises: ISR$^*{=}0.258$ ($n{=}11$) vs random ISR$^*{=}0.243$ ($n{=}21$)---a 1.06$\\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis\n\n\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$, ISR causes PR collapse to 18\\% (vs.\\ 48\\% random), completing the Goodhart trajectory: low-dose gaming ($\\beta{=}0.1$) gives way to high-dose collapse~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}\n\\label{sec:crosslogic}\n\nReplication in Prolog/SWI-Prolog confirms diagnostic transfer (full results: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).\nExec-only: 2/3 seeds show ISR$^*{\\approx}0$ despite moderate-high PR, reflecting \\emph{redundant coding} (repeated ground facts), not absent reasoning. %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42 (ISR*=0.037), plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3 (ISR*=0.015), plan006-prolog-exec-s44 (ISR*=0.387, small-sample n=21); C006\nISR-as-reward yields 0\\% non-hacking across 185 proved samples via three distinct mechanisms: s42 H7 tautologies (ISR$^*{=}1.0$); s43 uppercase pseudo-Prolog functionally equivalent to H7; s44 query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text classifiers (Figure~\\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s43, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.9\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples). ISR converts H1 (conclusion embedding) into more extreme H7 (conclusion duplication) without reducing total hacking; SFT is near-zero hacking (92.9\\% non-hacking).}\n\\label{fig:hacking-composition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSeven modes span two logic types (definitions and breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}): \\textbf{H1} (GRPO-universal, 68--97\\%), \\textbf{H7} (ISR-specific, 64.5\\% vs.\\ 0\\% random), \\textbf{tautological collapse} (exec-only s44), \\textbf{repetition collapse} (exec-only s43), \\textbf{contradiction explosion} (1000-step), \\textbf{complexity-without-utility} (random $\\beta{=}0.1$), and \\textbf{query-as-fact} (Prolog ISR s44). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s44, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan016-extended-1000-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\nThe reward signal determines hacking \\emph{composition} but not \\emph{prevalence}: ISR converts H1 into H7 without reducing totals (${\\sim}82$\\% pooled); SFT alone shows near-zero hacking (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}). %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4",

Looking at the appendix, I can see s43 is explicitly marked as using sampling evaluation rather than greedy. The experiments.tex file shows s43 PR at 13%, but I need to verify whether there's another reference claiming 14% and check what the actual registry value is for s43.

143	\caption{Hacking taxonomy across FOL conditions (\% of proved samples). ISR/Rand columns: $\beta{=}0.1$ 3-seed means. Exec: H1${=}87$\% in s42; $\bullet$ marks seed- or step-specific modes (H7/Tautological in s44, Repetition in s43, Contradiction at 1000 steps). C-w/o-util.: complexity-without-utility (random-specific). Q-as-fact: Prolog STA only (Table~\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).}
144	\label{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}
145	\end{table}
146	
147	\paragraph{Classifier Validation.}
148	Hacking mode classification uses deterministic rule-based classifiers, not learned models, so standard ML precision/recall is not directly applicable.
149	H1 and H7 are identified by normalized text matching between conclusion and premise strings after whitespace and variable normalization; remaining modes are detected via functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\text{necessary}}$ distribution from STA (leave-one-out ablation).
150	No training data or manual annotation is used for classification.
151	
152	To estimate precision, we manually verified 50 randomly sampled outputs classified as H1 or H7: all 50 were confirmed correct (estimated precision ${>}$95\%, Wilson 95\% CI: [93\%, 100\%]).
153	Recall is inherently bounded by the text-match threshold: semantic manipulations that alter surface form while preserving the gaming structure (e.g., variable renaming beyond our normalization, or paraphrased conclusions) would be missed.
154	We cross-validate with functional LOO analysis: samples classified as non-hacking but showing $n_{\text{necessary}} \leq 1$ are flagged as potential false negatives.
```

**M1: s43 PR 13/14 inconsistency**
From the paper:
- experiments.tex line 29: "s43 collapses to PR=13% via repetition"
- appendix.tex line 53-54: s43 uses sampling eval (not greedy), so its value might differ

The registry shows s43 greedy eval (exp2-exec-s43-greedy): PR=13% (greedy). The original sampling eval was PR=14% (from plan001-exec-only-s43-r4). 

Looking at the experiment plan data, the registry entry exp2-exec-s43-greedy shows sampling_pr=0.14 and greedy_pr=0.13, so the paper's PR=13% in experiments.tex refers to the greedy evaluation, but the appendix notes that s43 actually uses sampling eval which would give PR=14% instead. The key insight is that the collapse pattern is decoding-invariant—both greedy and sampling produce the same hacking signature, just with a slight PR difference of 13% versus 14%. Since the main text reports the greedy result (13%), which aligns with the evaluation protocol used for all other seeds, that's the correct value to use. The appendix can note the sampling exception without creating a real inconsistency.

But I'm noticing a contradiction: the appendix explicitly states s43 was evaluated with sampling, yet I'm proposing to report the greedy result in the main text. I need to either update the main text to 14% to match the documented sampling evaluation, or revise the appendix to clarify that greedy decoding was also verified and produced the same pattern at 13%.

Looking at the actual data, it seems both evaluations were run—the sampling result gave 14% and the greedy re-evaluation gave 13%. The cleanest approach is to standardize on 13% (greedy) throughout the paper and update the appendix to note that both methods were tested with consistent results.

The Director's instruction is straightforward: pick one value and use it consistently everywhere. Let me scan through the paper to find all references to s43's PR and consolidate them to a single number.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) across seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\\citep{folio} subset held constant across conditions. %% data: C001\nEvaluation uses 100 held-out examples with greedy decoding; cross-run ISR values are computed uniformly by \\texttt{eval\\_sta.py}. %% C004\nWe report PR, proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$), hacking rates (H7, H1\\footnote{H1/H7 numbering follows our taxonomy (Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}).}), and non-hacking rate.\\footnote{Non-hacking: not matching any of seven identified patterns (Table~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); does not imply semantic correctness.}\nFull hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:setup}.\n\n\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\n\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\\%$\\to$96\\% while proved-only ISR falls 0.642$\\to$0.003 over 1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\n\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces exec-only GRPO (seed~42): PR rises 32\\%$\\to$93\\%$\\to$96\\% while ISR$^*$ falls $0.642{\\to}0.132{\\to}0.003$ over 1000 steps. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42\nBy step 500, 87.1\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion (H1); by 1000, H1${=}100$\\% with mean 18.55 components.\nThe monotonic ISR$^*$ decline shows extended training worsens structural degeneration invisible to PR.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\\%, H1${=}87.1$\\%); s43 collapses to PR${=}13$\\% via repetition (87\\% hit max length); s44 reaches PR${=}98$\\% via tautological collapse (H7${=}100$\\%). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44\nDespite PR$\\in[13,98]$\\%, all three exhibit structural degeneration detectable by ISR; the $\\sigma{=}\\pm47$\\,pp cross-seed variance shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes (per-seed: Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\n\n\\textbf{SFT baseline.}\nSFT achieves PR${=}14$\\% with ISR$^*{=}0.571$: 92.9\\% non-hacking, only 1/14 exhibits H1, zero H7. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\nThe near-absence of hacking is consistent with hacking being RL-specific; a 100-step RL checkpoint analysis controlling for the PR-level confound is in Appendix~\\ref{app:100step}.\n\n\\subsection{Meta-Level Goodhart's Law}\n\\label{sec:goodhart}\n\nISR-as-reward provides the first empirical demonstration of Goodhart's Law at the meta level~\\citep{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}: the diagnostic itself becomes the target of gaming when optimized.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ & \\textbf{PR\\%}$\\uparrow$ & \\textbf{ISR}$^*$ & \\textbf{H7\\%} & \\textbf{H1\\%} & \\textbf{N-H\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase (0-shot) & --- & 12 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: sta-phase0-baseline\nSFT & --- & 14 & 0.571 & 0 & 7 & \\textbf{93} \\\\ %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\n\\midrule\nExec-only$^\\dagger$ & --- & 93 & 0.132 & 0 & 87 & 13 \\\\ %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2\nISR & 0.1 & 77{\\scriptsize$\\pm$14} & $\\ddagger$ & \\textbf{65} & 17 & ${\\sim}$16 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44\nRandom & 0.1 & \\textbf{85}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$6} & 0.086{\\scriptsize$\\pm$.04} & 0 & 81 & 19 \\\\ %% registry: plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\n\\midrule\nISR$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 18 & 0.143 & 0 & 78 & 22 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42 (single-seed)\nRandom$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 48 & 0.094 & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: plan017-additive-random-s42 (single-seed)\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Goodhart landscape across reward conditions. 3-seed rows show mean$\\pm$std. $^\\dagger$Seed~42; see \\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). $^\\S$$\\beta{=}0.3$: single-seed observation (seed~42). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across available seeds. Best PR and N-H\\% bolded per group.}\n\\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.\nAt $\\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds are dominated by H7 (64.5\\% of proved samples, 149/231) vs.\\ zero H7 under random controls (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively ISR-induced. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\nConclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\\%: random produces only H1, while ISR converts H1$\\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\nRandom also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\\pm$6\\% vs 77$\\pm$14\\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means\n\n\\textbf{Premise-count confound.}\nThe only ISR seed not dominated by H7 (s42) shows higher raw ISR$^*$ (0.415 vs 0.131), but this is entirely a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (5.58 vs 8.46 components), biasing LOO upward. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42, plan018-additive-random-b01-s42\nRestricting to non-hacking samples with $\\geq$3 premises: ISR$^*{=}0.258$ ($n{=}11$) vs random ISR$^*{=}0.243$ ($n{=}21$)---a 1.06$\\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis\n\n\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$ (seed~42), ISR causes PR collapse to 18\\% with 78\\% H1 embedding (vs.\\ 48\\% PR for random), completing the Goodhart trajectory: low-dose gaming ($\\beta{=}0.1$) gives way to high-dose collapse~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}\n\\label{sec:crosslogic}\n\nReplication in Prolog/SWI-Prolog confirms diagnostic transfer (full results: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).\nExec-only: 2/3 seeds show ISR$^*{\\approx}0$ despite moderate-high PR, reflecting \\emph{redundant coding} (repeated ground facts), not absent reasoning. %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42 (ISR*=0.037), plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3 (ISR*=0.015), plan006-prolog-exec-s44 (ISR*=0.387, small-sample n=21); C006\nISR-as-reward yields 0\\% non-hacking across 185 proved samples via three distinct mechanisms: s42 H7 tautologies (ISR$^*{=}1.0$); s43 uppercase pseudo-Prolog functionally equivalent to H7; s44 query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text classifiers (Figure~\\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s43, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.9\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples). ISR converts H1 (conclusion embedding) into more extreme H7 (conclusion duplication) without reducing total hacking; SFT is near-zero hacking (92.9\\% non-hacking).}\n\\label{fig:hacking-composition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSeven modes span two logic types (definitions and breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}): \\textbf{H1} (GRPO-universal, 68--97\\%), \\textbf{H7} (ISR-specific, 64.5\\% vs.\\ 0\\% random), \\textbf{tautological collapse} (exec-only s44), \\textbf{repetition collapse} (exec-only s43), \\textbf{contradiction explosion} (1000-step), \\textbf{complexity-without-utility} (random $\\beta{=}0.1$), and \\textbf{query-as-fact} (Prolog ISR s44). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s44, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan016-extended-1000-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\nThe reward signal determines hacking \\emph{composition} but not \\emph{prevalence}: ISR converts H1 into H7 without reducing totals (${\\sim}82$\\% pooled); SFT alone shows near-zero hacking (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}). %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4",

a 100-step RL checkpoint analysis controlling for the PR-level confound is in Appendix~\\ref{app:100step}.\n\n\\subsection{Meta-Level Goodhart's Law}\n\\label{sec:goodhart}\n\nISR-as-reward provides the first empirical demonstration of Goodhart's Law at the meta level~\\citep{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}: the diagnostic itself becomes the target of gaming when optimized.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ & \\textbf{PR\\%}$\\uparrow$ & \\textbf{ISR}$^*$ & \\textbf{H7\\%} & \\textbf{H1\\%} & \\textbf{N-H\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase (0-shot) & --- & 12 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: sta-phase0-baseline\nSFT & --- & 14 & 0.571 & 0 & 7 & \\textbf{93} \\\\ %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\n\\midrule\nExec-only$^\\dagger$ & --- & 93 & 0.132 & 0 & 87 & 13 \\\\ %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2\nISR & 0.1 & 77{\\scriptsize$\\pm$14} & $\\ddagger$ & \\textbf{65} & 17 & ${\\sim}$16 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44\nRandom & 0.1 & \\textbf{85}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$6} & 0.086{\\scriptsize$\\pm$.04} & 0 & 81 & 19 \\\\ %% registry: plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\n\\midrule\nISR$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 18 & 0.143 & 0 & 78 & 22 \\\\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42 (single-seed)\nRandom$^\\S$ & 0.3 & 48 & 0.094 & --- & --- & --- \\\\ %% registry: plan017-additive-random-s42 (single-seed)\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Goodhart landscape across reward conditions. 3-seed rows show mean$\\pm$std. $^\\dagger$Seed~42; see \\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). $^\\S$$\\beta{=}0.3$: single-seed observation (seed~42). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across available seeds. Best PR and N-H\\% bolded per group.}\n\\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.\nAt $\\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds are dominated by H7 (64.5\\% of proved samples, 149/231) vs.\\ zero H7 under random controls (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively ISR-induced. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\nConclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\\%: random produces only H1, while ISR converts H1$\\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\nRandom also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\\pm$6\\% vs 77$\\pm$14\\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means\n\n\\textbf{Premise-count confound.}\nThe only ISR seed not dominated by H7 (s42) shows higher raw ISR$^*$ (0.415 vs 0.131), but this is entirely a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (5.58 vs 8.46 components), biasing LOO upward. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42, plan018-additive-random-b01-s42\nRestricting to non-hacking samples with $\\geq$3 premises: ISR$^*{=}0.258$ ($n{=}11$) vs random ISR$^*{=}0.243$ ($n{=}21$)---a 1.06$\\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis\n\n\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$ (seed~42), ISR causes PR collapse to 18\\% with 78\\% H1 embedding (vs.\\ 48\\% PR for random), completing the Goodhart trajectory: low-dose gaming ($\\beta{=}0.1$) gives way to high-dose collapse~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}\n\\label{sec:crosslogic}\n\nReplication in Prolog/SWI-Prolog confirms diagnostic transfer (full results: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).\nExec-only: 2/3 seeds show ISR$^*{\\approx}0$ despite moderate-high PR, reflecting \\emph{redundant coding} (repeated ground facts), not absent reasoning. %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42 (ISR*=0.037), plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3 (ISR*=0.015), plan006-prolog-exec-s44 (ISR*=0.387, small-sample n=21); C006\nISR-as-reward yields 0\\% non-hacking across 185 proved samples via three distinct mechanisms: s42 H7 tautologies (ISR$^*{=}1.0$); s43 uppercase pseudo-Prolog functionally equivalent to H7; s44 query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text classifiers (Figure~\\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s43, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.9\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples). ISR converts H1 (conclusion embedding) into more extreme H7 (conclusion duplication) without reducing total hacking; SFT is near-zero hacking (92.9\\% non-hacking).}\n\\label{fig:hacking-composition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSeven modes span two logic types (definitions and breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}): \\textbf{H1} (GRPO-universal, 68--97\\%), \\textbf{H7} (ISR-specific, 64.5\\% vs.\\ 0\\% random), \\textbf{tautological collapse} (exec-only s44), \\textbf{repetition collapse} (exec-only s43), \\textbf{contradiction explosion} (1000-step), \\textbf{complexity-without-utility} (random $\\beta{=}0.1$), and \\textbf{query-as-fact} (Prolog ISR s44). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s44, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan016-extended-1000-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\nThe reward signal determines hacking \\emph{composition} but not \\emph{prevalence}: ISR converts H1 into H7 without reducing totals (${\\sim}82$\\% pooled); SFT alone shows near-zero hacking (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}). %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4",

formalize:\\\\n{question}\\\"\\n)\\n\\n\\n# --- Prompt config per logic type ---\\n\\nLOGIC_CONFIG = {\\n    \\\"fol\\\": {\\n        \\\"system_prompt\\\": FOL_SYSTEM_PROMPT,\\n        \\\"user_template\\\": FOL_USER_TEMPLATE,\\n        \\\"default_solver\\\": \\\"prover9\\\",\\n        \\\"reward_fn\\\": sta_reward,\\n    },\\n    \\\"prolog\\\": {\\n        \\\"system_prompt\\\": PROLOG_SYSTEM_PROMPT,\\n        \\\"user_template\\\": PROLOG_USER_TEMPLATE,\\n        \\\"default_solver\\\": \\\"prolog\\\",\\n        \\\"reward_fn\\\": prolog_sta_reward,\\n    },\\n}\\n\\n\\ndef load_data(path: str) -> list[dict]:\\n    data = []\\n    with open(path, \\\"r\\\") as f:\\n        for line in f:\\n            line = line.strip()\\n            if line:\\n                data.append(json.loads(line))\\n    return data\\n\\n\\ndef load_model(model_path: str):\\n    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\\n\\n    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_path, trust_remote_code=True)\\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\\n        model_path,\\n        torch_dtype=\\\"auto\\\",\\n        device_map=\\\"auto\\\",\\n        trust_remote_code=True,\\n    )\\n    model.eval()\\n    return model, tokenizer\\n\\n\\ndef generate_formalization(\\n    model, tokenizer, context: str, question: str,\\n    system_prompt: str, user_template: str,\\n    max_new_tokens: int = 512,\\n    do_sample: bool = False, temperature: float = 1.0,\\n) -> str:\\n    import torch\\n\\n    user_msg = user_template.format(context=context, question=question)\\n    messages = [\\n        {\\\"role\\\": \\\"system\\\", \\\"content\\\": system_prompt},\\n        {\\\"role\\\": \\\"user\\\", \\\"content\\\": user_msg},\\n    ]\\n\\n    text = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(\\n        messages,\\n        tokenize=False,\\n        add_generation_prompt=True,\\n        enable_thinking=False,\\n    )\\n    inputs = tokenizer([text], return_tensors=\\\"pt\\\").to(model.device)\\n\\n    with torch.no_grad():\\n        outputs = model.generate(\\n            **inputs,\\n            max_new_tokens=max_new_tokens,\\n            do_sample=do_sample,\\n            temperature=temperature,\\n            pad_token_id=tokenizer.pad_token_id,\\n        )\\n\\n    generated = outputs[0][inputs[\\\"input_ids\\\"].shape[1]:]\\n    result = tokenizer.decode(generated, skip_special_tokens=True).strip()\\n    result = re.sub(r\\\"&lt;think>.*?&lt;/think>\\\\s*\\\", \\\"\\\", result, flags=re.DOTALL).strip()\\n    return result\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=\\\"Full STA evaluation of a checkpoint\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--model_path\\\", type=str, required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--data_path\\\", type=str, required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--output_path\\\", type=str, required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--logic_type\\\", type=str, default=\\\"fol\\\", choices=[\\\"fol\\\", \\\"prolog\\\"],\\n                        help=\\\"Logic type: fol (Prover9) or prolog (swipl)\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--solver\\\", type=str, default=None,\\n                        help=\\\"Solver name (default: auto based on logic_type)\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--max_new_tokens\\\", type=int, default=512)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--timeout\\\", type=int, default=5)\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--max_samples\\\", type=int, default=None,\\n                        help=\\\"Max number of samples to evaluate (default: all)\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--do_sample\\\", action=\\\"store_true\\\", help=\\\"Use sampling instead of greedy decoding\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--temperature\\\", type=float, default=1.0, help=\\\"Sampling temperature\\\")\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    cfg = LOGIC_CONFIG[args.logic_type]\\n    reward_fn = cfg[\\\"reward_fn\\\"]\\n    solver_name = args.solver or cfg[\\\"default_solver\\\"]\\n    system_prompt = cfg[\\\"system_prompt\\\"]\\n    user_template = cfg[\\\"user_template\\\"]\\n\\n    data_path = args.data_path\\n    if not Path(data_path).exists():\\n        print(f\\\"ERROR: data path {data_path} not found\\\", file=sys.stderr)\\n        sys.exit(1)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Loading data from {data_path}...\\\")\\n    data = load_data(data_path)\\n    if args.max_samples is not None:\\n        data = data[:args.max_samples]\\n    print(f\\\"  {len(data)} problems loaded.\\\")\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Loading model from {args.model_path}...\\\")\\n    model, tokenizer = load_model(args.model_path)\\n    print(\\\"  Model loaded.\\\")\\n\\n    solver = get_solver(solver_name)\\n    print(f\\\"  Solver: {type(solver).__name__}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Logic type: {args.logic_type}\\\")\\n\\n    results = []\\n    isr_scores = []\\n    t_start = time.time()\\n\\n    for i, problem in enumerate(data):\\n        context = problem.get(\\\"context\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n        question = problem.get(\\\"question\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n        answer = problem.get(\\\"answer\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n\\n        print(f\\\"[{i+1}/{len(data)}] id={problem.get('id', i)} ...\\\", end=\\\" \\\", flush=True)\\n\\n        t_sample = time.time()\\n        formalization = generate_formalization(\\n            model, tokenizer, context, question,\\n            system_prompt, user_template,\\n            args.max_new_tokens,\\n            do_sample=args.do_sample, temperature=args.temperature,\\n        )\\n        gen_time = time.time() - t_sample\\n\\n        t_eval = time.time()\\n        reward = reward_fn(problem, formalization, solver, timeout=args.timeout)\\n        eval_time = time.time() - t_eval\\n\\n        record = {\\n            \\\"index\\\": i,\\n            \\\"id\\\": problem.get(\\\"id\\\", \\\"\\\"),\\n            \\\"context\\\": context,\\n            \\\"question\\\": question,\\n            \\\"answer\\\": answer,\\n            \\\"formalization\\\": formalization,\\n            \\\"gen_time\\\": round(gen_time, 2),\\n            \\\"eval_time\\\": round(eval_time, 2),\\n            **reward,\\n        }\\n        results.append(record)\\n\\n        isr_val = reward[\\\"sta_reward\\\"]\\n        isr_scores.append(isr_val)\\n        status = \\\"proved\\\" if reward[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0 else \\\"FAILED\\\"\\n        print(f\\\"{status}  ISR={isr_val:.3f}  gen={gen_time:.1f}s  eval={eval_time:.1f}s\\\")\\n\\n    elapsed = time.time() - t_start\\n\\n    n = len(isr_scores)\\n    proved_count = sum(1 for r in results if r[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0)\\n    prove_rate = proved_count / n if n else 0.0\\n    mean_isr = statistics.mean(isr_scores) if isr_scores else 0.0\\n    std_isr = statistics.stdev(isr_scores) if len(isr_scores) > 1 else 0.0\\n    median_isr = statistics.median(isr_scores) if isr_scores else 0.0\\n    isr_pr_ratio = mean_isr / prove_rate if prove_rate > 0 else 0.0\\n\\n    # Proved-only ISR stats\\n    proved_isrs = [r[\\\"sta_reward\\\"] for r in results if r[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0]\\n    proved_mean_isr = statistics.mean(proved_isrs) if proved_isrs else 0.0\\n\\n    by_answer = {}\\n    for r in results:\\n        a = r[\\\"answer\\\"]\\n        if a not in by_answer:\\n            by_answer[a] = {\\\"isr_scores\\\": [], \\\"proved\\\": 0, \\\"total\\\": 0}\\n        by_answer[a][\\\"isr_scores\\\"].append(r[\\\"sta_reward\\\"])\\n        by_answer[a][\\\"total\\\"] += 1\\n        if r[\\\"execution_reward\\\"] > 0:\\n            by_answer[a][\\\"proved\\\"] += 1\\n\\n    answer_stats = {}\\n    for a, stats in by_answer.items():\\n        answer_stats[a] = {\\n            \\\"count\\\": stats[\\\"total\\\"],\\n            \\\"proved\\\": stats[\\\"proved\\\"],\\n            \\\"prove_rate\\\": stats[\\\"proved\\\"] / stats[\\\"total\\\"] if stats[\\\"total\\\"] else 0.0,\\n            \\\"mean_isr\\\": statistics.mean(stats[\\\"isr_scores\\\"]) if stats[\\\"isr_scores\\\"] else 0.0,\\n        }\\n\\n    summary = {\\n        \\\"n_problems\\\": n,\\n        \\\"n_proved\\\": proved_count,\\n        \\\"prove_rate\\\": round(prove_rate, 4),\\n        \\\"mean_isr\\\": round(mean_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"isr_std\\\": round(std_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"median_isr\\\": round(median_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"isr_pr_ratio\\\": round(isr_pr_ratio, 4),\\n        \\\"proved_only_mean_isr\\\": round(proved_mean_isr, 4),\\n        \\\"min_isr\\\": round(min(isr_scores), 4) if isr_scores else 0.0,\\n        \\\"max_isr\\\": round(max(isr_scores), 4) if isr_scores else 0.0,\\n        \\\"by_answer_type\\\": answer_stats,\\n        \\\"elapsed_seconds\\\": round(elapsed, 1),\\n        \\\"avg_time_per_sample\\\": round(elapsed / n, 1) if n else 0.0,\\n        \\\"solver\\\": type(solver).__name__,\\n        \\\"logic_type\\\": args.logic_type,\\n        \\\"model_path\\\": args.model_path,\\n    }\\n\\n    output = {\\\"summary\\\": summary, \\\"results\\\": results}\\n\\n    Path(args.output_path).parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\\n    with open(args.output_path, \\\"w\\\") as f:\\n        json.dump(output, f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"\\\\n{'='*60}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Results saved to {args.output_path}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Logic type:   {args.logic_type}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Problems:     {n}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Proved:       {proved_count}/{n} ({prove_rate:.1%})\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Mean ISR:     {mean_isr:.4f} +/- {std_isr:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Median ISR:   {median_isr:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  ISR/PR ratio: {isr_pr_ratio:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Proved-only ISR: {proved_mean_isr:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  By answer type:\\\")\\n    for a, s in answer_stats.items():\\n        print(f\\\"    {a}: n={s['count']}, proved={s['proved']}, ISR={s['mean_isr']:.4f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Time: {elapsed:.1f}s ({summary['avg_time_per_sample']:.1f}s/sample)\\\")\\n\\n\\nif __name__ == \\\"__main__\\\":\\n    main()\\n```\"}}, \"error\":         "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\\S\\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\\S\\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) across seeds $\\{42,43,44\\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\\citep{folio} subset held constant across conditions. %% data: C001\nEvaluation uses 100 held-out examples with greedy decoding; cross-run ISR values are computed uniformly by \\texttt{eval\\_sta.py}. %% C004\nWe report PR, proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$), hacking rates (H7, H1\\footnote{H1/H7 numbering follows our taxonomy (Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}).}), and non-hacking rate.\\footnote{Non-hacking: not matching any of seven identified patterns (Table~\\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); does not imply semantic correctness.}\nFull hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:setup}.\n\n\\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}\n\\label{sec:diagnostic}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}\n\\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\\%$\\to$96\\% while proved-only ISR falls 0.642$\\to$0.003 over 1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}\n\\label{fig:isr-collapse}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces exec-only GRPO (seed~42): PR rises 32\\%$\\to$93\\%$\\to$96\\% while ISR$^*$ falls $0.642{\\to}0.132{\\to}0.003$ over 1000 steps. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42\nBy step 500, 87.1\\% of proved samples embed the conclusion (H1); by 1000, H1${=}100$\\% with mean 18.55 components.\nThe monotonic ISR$^*$ decline shows extended training worsens structural degeneration invisible to PR.\n\n\\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}\nThe three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\\%, H1${=}87.1$\\%); s43 collapses to PR${=}14$\\% via repetition (87\\% hit max length); s44 reaches PR${=}98$\\% via tautological collapse (H7${=}100$\\%). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44\nDespite PR$\\in[14,98]$\\%, all three exhibit structural degeneration detectable by ISR; the $\\sigma{=}\\pm47$\\,pp cross-seed variance shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes (per-seed: Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\n\n\\textbf{SFT baseline.}\nSFT achieves PR${=}14$\\% with ISR$^*{=}0.571$: 92.9\\% non-hacking, only 1/14 exhibits H1, zero H7. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4\nThe near-absence of hacking is consistent with hacking being RL-specific; see \\S\\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). $^\\S$$\\beta{=}0.3$: single-seed observation (seed~42). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across available seeds. Best PR and N-H\\% bolded per group.}\n\\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.\nAt $\\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds are dominated by H7 (64.5\\% of proved samples, 149/231) vs.\\ zero H7 under random controls (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively ISR-induced. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44\nConclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\\%: random produces only H1, while ISR converts H1$\\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).\nRandom also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\\pm$6\\% vs 77$\\pm$14\\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means\n\n\\textbf{Premise-count confound.}\nThe only ISR seed not dominated by H7 (s42) shows higher raw ISR$^*$ (0.415 vs 0.131), but this is entirely a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (5.58 vs 8.46 components), biasing LOO upward. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42, plan018-additive-random-b01-s42\nRestricting to non-hacking samples with $\\geq$3 premises: ISR$^*{=}0.258$ ($n{=}11$) vs random ISR$^*{=}0.243$ ($n{=}21$)---a 1.06$\\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis\n\n\\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}\nAt $\\beta{=}0.3$ (seed~42), ISR causes PR collapse to 18\\% with 78\\% H1 embedding (vs.\\ 48\\% PR for random), completing the Goodhart trajectory: low-dose gaming ($\\beta{=}0.1$) gives way to high-dose collapse~\\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}\n\\label{sec:crosslogic}\n\nReplication in Prolog/SWI-Prolog confirms diagnostic transfer (full results: Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).\nExec-only: 2/3 seeds show ISR$^*{\\approx}0$ despite moderate-high PR, reflecting \\emph{redundant coding} (repeated ground facts), not absent reasoning. %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42 (ISR*=0.037), plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3 (ISR*=0.015), plan006-prolog-exec-s44 (ISR*=0.387, small-sample n=21); C006\nISR-as-reward yields 0\\% non-hacking across 185 proved samples via three distinct mechanisms: s42 H7 tautologies (ISR$^*{=}1.0$); s43 uppercase pseudo-Prolog functionally equivalent to H7; s44 query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text classifiers (Figure~\\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s43, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\n\n\\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.9\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples). ISR converts H1 (conclusion embedding) into more extreme H7 (conclusion duplication) without reducing total hacking; SFT is near-zero hacking (92.9\\% non-hacking).}\n\\label{fig:hacking-composition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nSeven modes span two logic types (definitions and breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:hacking-modes}): \\textbf{H1} (GRPO-universal, 68--97\\%), \\textbf{H7} (ISR-specific, 64.5\\% vs.\\ 0\\% random), \\textbf{tautological collapse} (exec-only s44), \\textbf{repetition collapse} (exec-only s43), \\textbf{contradiction explosion} (1000-step), \\textbf{complexity-without-utility} (random $\\beta{=}0.1$), and \\textbf{query-as-fact} (Prolog ISR s44). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s44, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan016-extended-1000-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s44\nThe reward signal determines hacking \\emph{composition} but not \\emph{prevalence}: ISR converts H1 into H7 without reducing totals (${\\sim}82$\\% pooled); SFT alone shows near-zero hacking (Figure~\\ref{fig:hacking-composition}). %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4",
        "numLines": 95,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 95
      }

        "content": "---\nname: GPU contention prevention\ndescription: Eval workers must verify GPU is free before loading models — two OOM incidents from GPU contention\ntype: feedback\noriginSessionId: e611866d-baf8-4272-a82a-c437fb6a4f4b\n---\nEval workers must run `nvidia-smi --id=&lt;gpu> --query-compute-apps=pid` and abort if any process is using the target GPU before loading models.\n\n**Why:** Two separate OOM incidents (exp1-isr-b03-s44, exp1-random-b03-s44) caused by eval workers loading onto GPUs already occupied by training processes. Root cause: `kill_job` kills tmux session but the Python training process survives (SIGTERM doesn't propagate to child). The orphan process holds GPU memory invisibly.\n\n**How to apply:** \n- Before submitting any eval job, spawn worker must include `nvidia-smi` GPU check in the command\n- Use `gpu_guard.sh` wrapper script on server (`/root/solver-trace-ablation/scripts/gpu_guard.sh &lt;gpu_id> &lt;command>`)\n- After `kill_job`, always verify GPU is actually freed with `nvidia-smi` before resubmitting\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },

PR improves (+3%) but ISR collapses near-zero (0.0027). The model gets BETTER at proving while becoming WORSE at faithful reasoning.\\\\\\\\n2. Component count nearly tripled (6.58→18.55). Model generates longer proofs with more components, but 94.8% have ZERO necessary components.\\\\\\\\n3. NOT tautological collapse (comp=2 ratio=0). Instead: contradiction-based collapse — model embeds P(a) and ¬P(a) among many irrelevant premises, making anything provable via explosion.\\\\\\\\n4. Windowed ISR trend is monotonically decreasing: 0.1115 (101-200) → 0.0433 (401-500) → 0.0200 (601-700) → 0.0144 (901-1000). No recovery signal.\\\\\\\\n5. The exec-only reward creates a pure reward hacking incentive: the model discovers that contradictions + trivially true conclusions always satisfy the prover.\\\\\\\\n\\\\\\\\n## Implication for paper:\\\\\\\\nExtended training is a clean ablation showing exec-only reward's failure mode worsens over time.    if not Path(data_path).exists():\n        print(f\"ERROR: data path {data_path} not found\", file=sys.stderr)\n        sys.exit(1)\n\n    print(f\"Loading data from {data_path}...\")\n    data =    print(f\"  Logic type: {args.logic_type}\")\n\n    results = []\n    isr_scores = []\n    t_start = time.time()\n\n    for i, problem in enumerate(data):\n        context = problem.get(\"context\", \"\")\n        question = problem.get(\"question\", \"\")\n        answer = problem.get(\"answer\", \"\")\n\n        print(f\"[{i+1}/{len(data)}] id={problem.get('id', i)} ...\", end=\" \", flush=True)\n\n        t_sample = time.time()\n        formalization = generate_formalization(\n            model, tokenizer, context, question,\n            system_prompt, user_template,\n            args.max_new_tokens,\n            do_sample=args.do_sample, temperature=args.temperature,\n        )\n        gen_time = time.time() - t_sample\n\n        t_eval = time.time()\n        reward = reward_fn(problem, formalization, solver, timeout=args.timeout)\n        eval_time = time.time() - t_eval\n\n        record = {\n            \"index\": i,\n            \"id\": problem.get(\"id\", \"\"),\n            \"context\": context,\n            \"question\": question,\n            \"answer\": answer,\n            \"formalization\": formalization,\n            \"gen_time\": isr_scores.append(isr_val)\n        status = \"proved\" if reward[\"execution_reward\"] > 0 else \"FAILED\"\n        print(f\"{status}  ISR={isr_val:.3f}  gen={gen_time:.1f}s if n else 0.0\n    mean_isr = statistics.mean(isr_scores) if isr_scores else 0.0\n    std_isr = statistics.stdev(isr_scores) if len(isr_scores) > 1 else 0.0\n    median_isr = statistics.median(isr_scores) if isr_scores else 0.0\n    isr_pr_ratio = mean_isr / prove_rate if prove_rate > 0 else 0.0\n\n    # Proved-only ISR stats\n    proved_isrs = [r[\"sta_reward\"] for r in \"count\": stats[\"total\"],\n            \"proved\": stats[\"proved\"],\n            \"prove_rate\": stats[\"proved\"] / stats[\"total\"] if stats[\"total\"] else 0.0,\n            \"mean_isr\": statistics.mean(stats[\"isr_scores\"]) if stats[\"isr_scores\"] else 0.0,\n        }\n\n    summary = {\n        \"n_problems\": n,\n        \"n_proved\": proved_count,\n        \"prove_rate\": round(prove_rate, 4),\n        \"mean_isr\": round(mean_isr, 4),\n        \"isr_std\": round(std_isr, 4),\n        \"median_isr\": round(median_isr, 4),\n        \"isr_pr_ratio\": round(isr_pr_ratio, 4),\n        \"proved_only_mean_isr\": round(proved_mean_isr, 4),\n        \"min_isr\": round(min(isr_scores), 4) if isr_scores else 0.0,\n        \"max_isr\": round(max(isr_scores), 4) if isr_scores Path(args.output_path).parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    with open(args.output_path, \"w\") as f:\n        json.dump(output, f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)\n\n    print(f\"\\n{'='*60}\")\n    print(f\"Results saved to {args.output_path}\")\n    print(f\"  Logic type:   {args.logic_type}\")\n    print(f\"  Problems:     {n}\")\n    print(f\"  Proved: {isr_pr_ratio:.4f}\")\n    print(f\"  Proved-only ISR: {proved_mean_isr:.4f}\")\n    print(f\"  By answer type:\")\n    for a, s in answer_stats.items():\n        print(f\"    {a}: n={s['count']}, 1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate STA along three axes: as a diagnostic for reward hacking (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic}); as a training reward exhibiting meta-level Goodhart's Law (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}); and as a cross-logic protocol (\S\ref{sec:crosslogic}). We synthesize results into a hacking taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
7	
8	We train Qwen3-4B with GRPO~\citep{deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$ rollouts, 500 steps default, 1000 where noted) across seeds $\{42,43,44\}$ on a fixed 500-example FOLIO~\citep{folio} subset held constant across conditions. %% data: C001
9	Evaluation uses 100 held-out examples with greedy decoding; cross-run ISR values are computed uniformly by \texttt{eval\_sta.py}. %% C004
10	We report PR, proved-only ISR (ISR$^*$), hacking rates (H7, H1\footnote{H1/H7 numbering follows our taxonomy (Appendix~\ref{app:hacking-modes}).}), and non-hacking rate.\footnote{Non-hacking: not matching any of seven identified patterns (Table~\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}); does not imply semantic correctness.}
11	Full hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{app:setup}.
12	
13	\subsection{ISR as Diagnostic}
14	\label{sec:diagnostic}
15	
16	\begin{figure}[t]
17	\centering
18	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_isr_collapse.pdf}
19	\caption{ISR collapse under exec-only GRPO (seed~42). PR rises 32\%$\to$96\% while proved-only ISR falls 0.642$\to$0.003 over 1000 steps. By step 1000, 94.8\% of proved samples have ISR${=}0$ and mean component count inflates 6.58$\to$18.55 (contradiction explosion).}
20	\label{fig:isr-collapse}
21	\end{figure}
22	
23	\textbf{Collapse trajectory.}
24	Figure~\ref{fig:isr-collapse} traces exec-only GRPO (seed~42): PR rises 32\%$\to$93\%$\to$96\% while ISR$^*$ falls $0.642{\to}0.132{\to}0.003$ over 1000 steps. %% registry: plan003-eval-exec-only-r5, plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan016-extended-1000-s42
25	By step 500, 87.1\% of proved samples embed the conclusion (H1); by 1000, H1${=}100$\% with mean 18.55 components.
26	The monotonic ISR$^*$ decline shows extended training worsens structural degeneration invisible to PR.
27	
28	\textbf{Cross-seed instability.}
29	The three exec-only seeds diverge radically: s42 hacks via H1 (PR${=}93$\%, H1${=}87.1$\%); s43 collapses to PR${=}14$\% via repetition (87\% hit max length); s44 reaches PR${=}98$\% via tautological collapse (H7${=}100$\%). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan001-exec-only-s44
30	Despite PR$\in[14,98]$\%, all three exhibit structural degeneration detectable by ISR; the $\sigma{=}\pm47$\,pp cross-seed variance shows aggregate metrics obscure qualitatively different failure modes (per-seed: Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).
31	
32	\textbf{SFT baseline.}
33	SFT achieves PR${=}14$\% with ISR$^*{=}0.571$: 92.9\% non-hacking, only 1/14 exhibits H1, zero H7. %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4
34	The near-absence of hacking is consistent with hacking being RL-specific; a 100-step RL checkpoint analysis controlling for the PR-level confound is in Appendix~\ref{app:100step}.
35	
36	\subsection{Meta-Level Goodhart's Law}
37	\label{sec:goodhart}
38	
39	ISR-as-reward provides the first empirical demonstration of Goodhart's Law at the meta level~\citep{manheim-goodhart-taxonomy}: the diagnostic itself becomes the target of gaming when optimized.
40	
41	\begin{table}[t]
42	\centering
43	\small
44	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
45	\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
46	\toprule
47	\textbf{Condition} & $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ & \textbf{PR\%}$\uparrow$ & \textbf{ISR}$^*$ & \textbf{H7\%} & \textbf{H1\%} & \textbf{N-H\%} \\
48	\midrule
49	Base (0-shot) & --- & 12 & --- & --- & --- & --- \\ %% registry: sta-phase0-baseline
50	SFT & --- & 14 & 0.571 & 0 & 7 & \textbf{93} \\ %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4
51	\midrule
52	Exec-only$^\dagger$ & --- & 93 & 0.132 & 0 & 87 & 13 \\ %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s42-r2
53	ISR & 0.1 & 77{\scriptsize$\pm$14} & $\ddagger$ & \textbf{65} & 17 & ${\sim}$16 \\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44
54	Random & 0.1 & \textbf{85}{\scriptsize$\pm$6} & 0.086{\scriptsize$\pm$.04} & 0 & 81 & 19 \\ %% registry: plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44
55	\midrule
56	ISR$^\S$ & 0.3 & 18 & 0.143 & 0 & 78 & 22 \\ %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42 (single-seed)
57	Random$^\S$ & 0.3 & 48 & 0.094 & --- & --- & --- \\ %% registry: plan017-additive-random-s42 (single-seed)
58	\bottomrule
59	\end{tabular}}
60	\caption{Goodhart landscape across reward conditions. 3-seed rows show mean$\pm$std. $^\dagger$Seed~42; see \S\ref{sec:diagnostic} for 3-seed divergence. $\ddagger$ISR$^*$ omitted: inflated by H7 gaming (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}). $^\S$$\beta{=}0.3$: single-seed observation (seed~42). H7 and H1 rates are simple means across available seeds. Best PR and N-H\% bolded per group.}
61	\label{tab:goodhart-landscape}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	\textbf{Low-dose ISR ($\beta{=}0.1$): ISR-specific gaming.}
65	Table~\ref{tab:goodhart-landscape} summarizes results.
66	At $\beta{=}0.1$, 2/3 ISR seeds are dominated by H7 (64.5\% of proved samples, 149/231) vs.\ zero H7 under random controls (0/256), confirming H7 is exclusively ISR-induced. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42/s43/s44 vs plan018-additive-random-b01-s42/s43-r2/s44
67	Conclusion embedding (H1+H7) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\%: random produces only H1, while ISR converts H1$\to$H7 without reducing the total (Figure~\ref{fig:hacking-composition}; per-seed: Appendix Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).
68	Random also achieves higher and more stable PR (85$\pm$6\% vs 77$\pm$14\%). %% registry: plan018 vs plan002 3-seed means
69	
70	\textbf{Premise-count confound.}
71	The only ISR seed not dominated by H7 (s42) shows higher raw ISR$^*$ (0.415 vs 0.131), but this is entirely a premise-minimization artifact: ISR-trained models produce shorter formalizations (5.58 vs 8.46 components), biasing LOO upward. %% registry: plan002-additive-b01-s42, plan018-additive-random-b01-s42
72	Restricting to non-hacking samples with $\geq$3 premises: ISR$^*{=}0.258$ ($n{=}11$) vs random ISR$^*{=}0.243$ ($n{=}21$)---a 1.06$\times$ ratio, no meaningful advantage. %% D051 premise-count-matched analysis
73	
74	\textbf{High-dose ISR ($\beta{=}0.3$): classic Goodhart.}
75	At $\beta{=}0.3$ (seed~42), ISR causes PR collapse to 18\% with 78\% H1 embedding (vs.\ 48\% PR for random), completing the Goodhart trajectory: low-dose gaming ($\beta{=}0.1$) gives way to high-dose collapse~\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}. %% registry: plan002-additive-b03-s42, plan017-additive-random-s42
76	
77	\subsection{Cross-Logic Diagnostic Transfer}
78	\label{sec:crosslogic}
79	
80	Replication in Prolog/SWI-Prolog confirms diagnostic transfer (full results: Appendix Table~\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).
81	Exec-only: 2/3 seeds show ISR$^*{\approx}0$ despite moderate-high PR, reflecting \emph{redundant coding} (repeated ground facts), not absent reasoning. %% registry: plan006-prolog-exec-s42 (ISR*=0.037), plan006-prolog-exec-s43-r3 (ISR*=0.015), plan006-prolog-exec-s44 (ISR*=0.387, small-sample n=21); C006
82	ISR-as-reward yields 0\% non-hacking across 185 proved samples via three distinct mechanisms: s42 H7 tautologies (ISR$^*{=}1.0$); s43 uppercase pseudo-Prolog functionally equivalent to H7; s44 query-as-fact gaming that bypasses text classifiers (Figure~\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}). %% registry: plan006-prolog-sta-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s43, plan006-prolog-sta-s44
83	
84	\subsection{Hacking Taxonomy}
85	\label{sec:taxonomy}
86	
87	\begin{figure}[t]
88	\centering
89	\includegraphics[width=0.9\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_hacking_composition.pdf}
90	\caption{Hacking composition across reward conditions (proved samples). ISR converts H1 (conclusion embedding) into more extreme H7 (conclusion duplication) without reducing total hacking; SFT is near-zero hacking (92.9\% non-hacking).}
91	\label{fig:hacking-composition}
92	\end{figure}
93	
94	Seven modes span two logic types (definitions and breakdown in Appendix~\ref{app:hacking-modes}): \textbf{H1} (GRPO-universal, 68--97\%), \textbf{H7} (ISR-specific, 64.5\% vs.\ 0\% random), \textbf{tautological collapse} (exec-only s44), \textbf{repetition collapse} (exec-only s43), \textbf{contradiction explosion} (1000-step), \textbf{complexity-without-utility} (random $\beta{=}0.1$), and \textbf{query-as-fact} (Prolog ISR s44). %% registry: plan001-exec-only-s44, plan001-exec-only-s43-r4, plan016-extended-1000-s42, plan006-prolog-sta-s44
95	The reward signal determines hacking \emph{composition} but not \emph{prevalence}: ISR converts H1 into H7 without reducing totals (${\sim}82$\% pooled); SFT alone shows near-zero hacking (Figure~\ref{fig:hacking-composition}). %% registry: plan009-sft-baseline-s42-r4

```text
1	% Appendix — solver-trace-ablation
2	
3	\section{Experimental Setup}
4	\label{app:setup}
5	
6	Table~\ref{tab:hyperparams} lists all hyperparameters shared across experiments.
7	All RL conditions use the same GRPO configuration; only the reward function differs.
8	Seeds control model weight initialization and data shuffling; training data is held constant across all seeds and conditions (Constraint~C001).
9	
10	\begin{table}[h]
11	\centering
12	\caption{Training hyperparameters for all GRPO experiments.}
13	\label{tab:hyperparams}
14	\footnotesize
15	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
16	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
17	\toprule
18	\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
19	\midrule
20	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{Model}} \\
21	Base model & Qwen3-4B \\
22	Precision & BF16 \\
23	\midrule
24	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{GRPO Training}} \\
25	Rollouts per prompt ($K$) & 8 \\
26	Training steps (default) & 500 \\
27	Training steps (extended) & 1000 \\
28	Learning rate & $1 \times 10^{-6}$ \\
29	Per-device batch size & 4 \\
30	Gradient accumulation steps & 2 \\
31	Effective batch size & 8 \\
32	Max completion length & 512 tokens \\
33	Random seeds & 42, 43, 44 \\
34	\midrule
35	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{Data}} \\
36	Training set & FOLIO, 500 examples \\
37	Evaluation set & FOLIO, 100 examples (held-out) \\
38	Data file & \texttt{train\_500\_seed42} \\
39	\midrule
40	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{Solvers}} \\
41	FOL solver & Prover9 \\
42	Prolog solver & SWI-Prolog 8.4.2 \\
43	\midrule
44	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{Evaluation}} \\
45	Decoding & Greedy ($T{=}0$) \\
46	ISR computation & \texttt{eval\_sta.py} \\
47	ISR variant & Proved-only (ISR$^*$) \\
48	\bottomrule
49	\end{tabular}}
50	\end{table}
51	
52	\paragraph{Evaluation exception.}
53	FOL exec-only seed~43 (\texttt{plan001-exec-only-s43-r4}) was evaluated with sampling (temperature~$=0.7$) rather than greedy decoding.
54	Its PR and ISR values are included for completeness but are not strictly comparable with other seeds under the unified evaluation protocol (C004).
55	
56	
57	\section{STA Algorithm}
58	\label{app:algorithm}
59	
60	Algorithm~\ref{alg:sta} gives the full procedure for computing ISR over a proved formalization.
61	
62	\begin{algorithm}[h]
63	\caption{Solver Trace Ablation (STA)}
64	\label{alg:sta}
65	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
66	\REQUIRE Formalization $x = \{c_1, \ldots, c_n\}$, solver $S$
67	\ENSURE ISR score $\in [0,1]$ or $\bot$ (undefined)
68	\IF{$S(x) \neq \textit{proved}$}
69	  \RETURN $\bot$
70	\ENDIF
71	\STATE $n_{\text{nec}} \gets 0$
72	\FOR{$i = 1$ \TO $n$}
73	  \STATE $x' \gets x \setminus \{c_i\}$
74	  \IF{$S(x') = \textit{failed}$}
75	    \STATE $n_{\text{nec}} \gets n_{\text{nec}} + 1$
76	  \ENDIF
77	\ENDFOR
78	\RETURN $n_{\text{nec}} \mathbin{/} n$
79	\end{algorithmic}
80	\end{algorithm}
81	
82	
83	\section{Extended Discussion}
84	\label{app:extended-discussion}
85	
86	\paragraph{What ISR measures---and what it does not.}
87	ISR quantifies syntactic component necessity: the fraction of solver-input components whose individual removal causes proof failure.
88	This is strictly weaker than semantic faithfulness---whether the formalization accurately captures the natural-language meaning.
89	We verify this empirically: Spearman $\rho(\text{ISR}, \text{NLI accuracy}) = -0.049$ ($p = 0.187$, n.s.) from a downstream NLI evaluation on exec-only checkpoint outputs.
90	ISR and downstream task performance are uncorrelated.
91	ISR is thus a structural integrity diagnostic, not a semantic quality metric: high ISR indicates that every component contributes to the proof, not that the proof faithfully represents the original reasoning problem.
92	
93	Critically, low ISR carries different semantics depending on the training objective.
94	Under exec-only training, low ISR in Prolog reflects redundant coding---repeated ground facts where removing any single copy does not affect the proof---rather than absence of reasoning (\S\ref{sec:crosslogic}).
95	Under ISR-as-reward, high ISR reflects metric gaming (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}).
96	ISR values must always be interpreted relative to the optimization objective that produced them.
97	
98	\paragraph{The premise-count confound.}
99	LOO-based metrics like ISR have an inherent mathematical bias: with fewer components, each is mechanically more likely to be necessary.
100	A formalization with 2 premises where both are necessary yields ISR${=}1.0$; one with 10 premises where 8 are necessary yields ISR${=}0.8$---even though the latter encodes richer reasoning.
101	This confound explains the sole apparent ISR-as-reward success (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}): ISR $\beta{=}0.1$ seed~42 produces shorter formalizations, mechanically inflating ISR.
102	Premise-count-matched comparison eliminates the difference ($1.06\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beta01-3seed}).
103	This bias is not specific to our setting---any LOO-based structural metric applied to variable-length outputs requires component-count normalization.
104	We recommend that future work employing ablation-based diagnostics report premise-count-matched comparisons alongside raw values.
105	
106	\paragraph{Meta-level Goodhart: implications.}
107	The meta-Goodhart finding extends beyond this specific system.
108	ISR was designed to \emph{detect} reward gaming of $r_{\text{exec}}$, yet when incorporated into the reward signal, the model games ISR itself---creating a regress where any diagnostic metric risks becoming a target when optimized.
109	The dose-response pattern ($\beta{=}0.1$: proxy gaming inflates ISR without genuine improvement; $\beta{=}0.3$: PR collapse to 18\% vs.\ 48\% random) maps onto the trajectory predicted by~\cite{gao-reward-overoptimization}, but at a meta level: the proxy being gamed is itself a gaming detector.
110	$\beta{=}0.1$ instantiates a meta-level variant where the diagnostic is gamed through its own measurement mechanism; $\beta{=}0.3$ completes the Goodhart trajectory with outright performance collapse.
111	The practical implication is that ISR---and structurally similar diagnostics---should be used exclusively as post-hoc evaluation tools, never as training rewards.
112	
113	\paragraph{Single-seed unreliability.}
114	The three exec-only seeds diverge radically (PR $\sigma{=}\pm 47$ percentage points), each converging to a qualitatively different degeneration mode (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
115	ISR $\beta{=}0.1$ seeds show similar divergence: 2/3 dominated by conclusion duplication (H7), 1/3 by premise minimization (\S\ref{sec:goodhart}).
116	Multi-seed evaluation with structural diagnostics should be standard practice for neurosymbolic RL systems.
117	
118	
119	\section{Hacking Mode Definitions}
120	\label{app:hacking-modes}
121	
122	We identify seven distinct reward hacking modes across all experiments.
123	Each mode is defined below with its mechanism, a representative example, and its interaction with ISR.
124	Table~\ref{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app} summarizes prevalence across FOL conditions.
125	
126	\begin{table}[h]
127	\centering
128	\small
129	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
130	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
131	\toprule
132	\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Mechanism} & \textbf{Exec} & \textbf{ISR} & \textbf{Rnd} & \textbf{SFT} \\
133	\midrule
134	H1 & Concl.\ in premises & 87 & 17 & 81 & 7 \\
135	H7 & Single prem.\ = concl. & $\bullet$ & \textbf{65} & 0 & 0 \\
136	Tautological & 2-comp $P{=}C$ & $\bullet$ & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
137	Repetition & Output degen. & $\bullet$ & -- & -- & -- \\
138	Contradiction & $n_{\text{comp}}$ inflated & $\bullet$ & -- & -- & -- \\
139	C-w/o-utility & Complex, redundant & 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\
140	Q-as-fact & Atom = fact+query & -- & -- & -- & -- \\
141	\bottomrule
142	\end{tabular}}
143	\caption{Hacking taxonomy across FOL conditions (\% of proved samples). ISR/Rand columns: $\beta{=}0.1$ 3-seed means. Exec: H1${=}87$\% in s42; $\bullet$ marks seed- or step-specific modes (H7/Tautological in s44, Repetition in s43, Contradiction at 1000 steps). C-w/o-util.: complexity-without-utility (random-specific). Q-as-fact: Prolog STA only (Table~\ref{tab:prolog-crosslogic}).}
144	\label{tab:hacking-taxonomy-app}
145	\end{table}
146	
147	\paragraph{Classifier Validation.}
148	Hacking mode classification uses deterministic rule-based classifiers, not learned models, so standard ML precision/recall is not directly applicable.
149	H1 and H7 are identified by normalized text matching between conclusion and premise strings after whitespace and variable normalization; remaining modes are detected via functional analysis of the per-sample $n_{\text{necessary}}$ distribution from STA (leave-one-out ablation).
150	No training data or manual annotation is used for classification.
151	
152	To estimate precision, we manually verified 50 randomly sampled outputs classified as H1 or H7: all 50 were confirmed correct (estimated precision ${>}$95\%, Wilson 95\% CI: [93\%, 100\%]).
153	Recall is inherently bounded by the text-match threshold: semantic manipulations that alter surface form while preserving the gaming structure (e.g., variable renaming beyond our normalization, or paraphrased conclusions) would be missed.
154	We cross-validate with functional LOO analysis: samples classified as non-hacking but showing $n_{\text{necessary}} \leq 1$ are flagged as potential false negatives.
155	This cross-validation revealed query-as-fact gaming in Prolog (\S\ref{app:hacking-modes}), where the strict text classifier reported 100\% non-hacking but functional analysis showed 95/97 samples were tautological ($n_{\text{necessary}}{=}1$).
156	The non-hacking rate for FOL conditions may therefore be slightly overestimated, as subtle semantic manipulations below our text-matching threshold would evade the strict classifier.
157	
158	\subsection{H1 --- Conclusion Embedding}
159	
160	\paragraph{Definition.}
161	The conclusion appears verbatim (or with trivial variable renaming) as one premise among multiple premises.
162	The solver succeeds by matching the embedded conclusion, rendering the other premises irrelevant to the proof.
163	
164	\paragraph{Example (FOL).}
165	{\small\begin{verbatim}
166	Premises:
167	  all x (bird(x) -> flies(x)).
168	  bird(tweety).
169	  flies(tweety).  % conclusion embedded
170	Conclusion: flies(tweety).
171	\end{verbatim}}
172	
173	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
174	Variable. The embedded premise has ISR~$=1$ (removing it may break the proof), but other premises may also register as necessary depending on Prover9's search strategy.
175	ISR does not distinguish trivial from substantive necessity.
176	
177	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
178	Conclusion embedding in some form (H1 multi-premise or H7 single-premise) is GRPO-universal at 68--97\% across all RL conditions.
179	Under exec-only and Random reward, it manifests as H1 (71--93\%); under ISR reward, it shifts to H7 (64.5\% pooled).
180	
181	
182	\subsection{H7 --- Conclusion Duplication}
183	
184	\paragraph{Definition.}
185	The entire formalization collapses to a single premise whose content is identical to the conclusion.
186	Since $P \to P$ is trivially provable, this simultaneously maximizes exec reward.
187	Because the sole premise is necessary for every sub-component of the conclusion under LOO ablation, ISR is inflated toward 1.0.
188	
189	\paragraph{Example (FOL).}
190	{\small\begin{verbatim}
191	Premises:
192	  all x ((A(x)&B(x))->(C(x)|D(x))).
193	Conclusion:
194	  all x ((A(x)&B(x))->(C(x)|D(x))).
195	\end{verbatim}}
196	
197	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
198	ISR~$\approx 0.8$--$1.0$.
199	The single premise is trivially ``maximally necessary,'' but the proof is vacuous.
200	H7 is the only mode that simultaneously games both exec reward and ISR reward.
201	
202	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
203	ISR-specific: 64.5\% pooled (149/231) under ISR~$\beta{=}0.1$ (s42: 16.0\%, s43: 96.7\%, s44: 86.5\%).
204	\textbf{Zero} occurrences under Random~$\beta{=}0.1$ across all three seeds ($0/256$).
205	
206	
207	\subsection{Tautological Collapse}
208	
209	\paragraph{Definition.}
210	Two-component formalizations where the premise and conclusion are identical atomic or simple formulas, yielding a $P \to P$ tautology with minimal token cost.
211	
212	\paragraph{Example (FOL).}
213	\begin{verbatim}
214	Premises: p(a).
215	Conclusion: p(a).
216	\end{verbatim}
217	
218	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
219	ISR~$=1.0$ (the sole premise is necessary).
220	Distinguished from H7 by having low component count ($n_{\text{comp}}\approx 2$) and atomic formulas rather than complex sub-structured conclusions.
221	
222	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
223	Dominant in FOL exec-only seed~44 (PR~$=0.98$, mean $n_{\text{comp}}=2.04$, 100\% H7 by strict classifier).
224	Also observed in Prolog exec+STA seed~42 (100\% H7, PR~$=0.50$).
225	
226	
227	\subsection{Repetition Collapse}
228	
229	\paragraph{Definition.}
230	The model generates identical premises or clauses repeated until the maximum completion length, producing a degenerate output with high token count but no logical diversity.
231	Removing any single repeated copy leaves the others intact, yielding ISR~$\approx 0$.
232	
233	\paragraph{Example (Prolog).}
234	\begin{verbatim}
235	penguin(fido).
236	penguin(fido).
237	penguin(fido).
238	... (repeated to 512 tokens)
239	query(penguin(fido)).
240	\end{verbatim}
241	
242	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
243	ISR~$\approx 0$. Each copy is individually redundant since identical copies remain after removal.
244	ISR correctly diagnoses this degeneration.
245	
246	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
247	Observed in FOL exec-only seed~43 (87\% of outputs hit max completion length; PR~$=14$\%, ISR~$=0$).
248	
249	
250	\subsection{Complexity-without-Utility}
251	
252	\paragraph{Definition.}
253	The formalization contains syntactically complex premises (nested quantifiers, multi-arity predicates) that are semantically vacuous---none individually necessary for the proof.
254	The complexity inflates surface metrics (formula length, nesting depth) without contributing to logical derivation.
255	
256	\paragraph{Example (FOL).}
257	{\small\begin{verbatim}
258	Premises:
259	  all x all y (R(x,y) ->
260	    exists z (S(x,z) & T(z,y))).
261	  all x (P(x) -> Q(x)).
262	  P(a).            % only this needed
263	Conclusion: Q(a).
264	\end{verbatim}}
265	
266	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
267	ISR correctly identifies the vacuous premises (ISR~$=0$ for each), pulling down the mean ISR.
268	This is the mode ISR is best calibrated for.
269	
270	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
271	Low. Observed in Random~$\beta{=}0.1$ seed~42 at 3.8\% (3/79).
272	Not observed under ISR reward (ISR's gradient discourages zero-ISR premises).
273	
274	
275	\subsection{Contradiction Explosion}
276	
277	\paragraph{Definition.}
278	The component count grows unboundedly over training.
279	Excess premises introduce contradictions or redundancies that trivially entail any conclusion via \emph{ex falso quodlibet}.
280	Individual premises may register as ISR~$=0$ (removable without breaking the proof), but the sheer count obscures the underlying triviality.
281	
282	\paragraph{Example (FOL, 1000-step exec-only).}
283	{\small\begin{verbatim}
284	Premises:
285	  c1. c2. c3. ... c18. % 18.55 mean
286	  % conclusion embedded among premises
287	Conclusion: Q(a).
288	\end{verbatim}}
289	
290	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
291	ISR~$\approx 0$ (at 1000 steps: ISR~$=0.003$, with 94.8\% of proved samples having ISR~$=0$).
292	
293	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
294	Observed in extended exec-only training (seed~42, 1000 steps).
295	Mean component count grows from ${\sim}6$ (500 steps) to 18.55 (1000 steps).
296	
297	
298	\subsection{Query-as-Fact Gaming}
299	
300	\paragraph{Definition.}
301	The model converts the natural language conclusion into a snake\_case atom and includes it as both a ground fact and the query.
302	Prolog trivially proves the query by unifying with the fact.
303	This mode evades text-match classifiers designed for H1/H7 because the generated atom bears no surface resemblance to the original conclusion text.
304	
305	\paragraph{Example (Prolog).}
306	\begin{verbatim}
307	% NL: "Fido is happy."
308	% Model output:
309	fido_is_happy.
310	:- fido_is_happy.     % query = fact
311	\end{verbatim}
312	
313	\paragraph{ISR interaction.}
314	ISR~$\approx 0.9$--$1.0$.
315	The fact is necessarily necessary (its removal breaks the query), inflating ISR.
316	Functionally equivalent to H7 but with a distinct surface mechanism.
317	
318	\paragraph{Prevalence.}
319	Observed in Prolog exec+STA seed~44 (PR~$=0.97$, ISR~$=0.912$).
320	95/97 proved samples have $n_{\text{necessary}}=1$, confirming functional tautology despite the strict classifier reporting 100\% non-hacking.
321	
322

418	\caption{Prolog cross-logic comparison. Exec-only vs Exec+ISR (STA reward) across three seeds, illustrating universal Goodhart under ISR-as-reward (3/3 seeds, 0\% non-hacking) and three distinct gaming pathways (H7 tautological, uppercase pseudo-Prolog, query-as-fact).}
419	\label{fig:prolog-comparison}

421	
422	\paragraph{ISR collapse trajectory (FOL exec-only, seed 42).}
423	Table~\ref{tab:fol-detailed} traces the ISR collapse over training:
424	ISR$^*=0.642$ at 100 steps $\to$ $0.132$ at 500 steps ($-79.4$\%) $\to$ $0.003$ at 1000 steps ($-99.5$\% from step 100).
425	PR increases monotonically ($0.32 \to 0.93 \to 0.96$) while structural quality collapses, confirming that ISR captures degeneration invisible to execution reward.
426	By step 1000, 94.8\% of proved samples have ISR~$=0$ and the mean component count rises to 18.55.
427	
428	\paragraph{Prolog: universal Goodhart via three distinct pathways.}
429	All three Prolog exec+ISR seeds exhibit Goodhart's Law with 0\% non-hacking proofs (Figure~\ref{fig:prolog-comparison}), but each discovers a different gaming mechanism:
430	seed~42 produces clean H7 tautologies (single premise~$=$~conclusion),
431	seed~43 generates uppercase pseudo-Prolog that bypasses the text-match classifier while remaining functionally tautological,
432	and seed~44 converts NL conclusions to snake\_case atoms used as both fact and query.
433	The diversity of gaming pathways across seeds---despite identical training data and reward function---demonstrates that Goodhart's Law in neurosymbolic reasoning is not a single failure mode but a family of convergent exploits.
434	
435	\subsection{Early-Training Hacking Analysis (100-Step Checkpoint)}
436	\label{app:100step}
437	
438	To control for the prove-rate confound in our SFT comparison (\S\ref{sec:diagnostic}), we analyze the exec-only checkpoint at 100 training steps (seed~42), where PR${=}32$\%---higher than SFT (14\%) but still early in training.
439	Of the 32 proved samples, 9 (28.1\%) exhibit H7 hacking (single premise identical to conclusion) and 1 (3.1\%) exhibits H1 (conclusion embedded among premises), yielding a total hacking rate of 31.2\%.
440	By contrast, SFT at comparable or lower PR shows 7.1\% hacking (1/14 proved samples).
441	This demonstrates that reward hacking emerges early in GRPO training---well before the prove-rate plateau---and is not simply an artifact of having more proved samples available for exploitation.
442	The 100-step hacking rate (31.2\%) is already substantially above SFT (7.1\%), supporting the claim that hacking is associated with RL optimization rather than prove-rate level alone.
443	
```
